```json
    "newString": "For DeepSeek, the effect size increases monotonically from medium ($d{=}0.61$) at $s{=}0.15$ to large ($d{=}1.13$) at $s{=}0.30$, with all bootstrap CIs for $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$ excluding zero (Table~\\ref{tab:cohens-d}). % exp_id: bf16_ds_imp_s015..s030; per-problem paired B=10000",
    "originalFile": "\n\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\nWe present our empirical findings in five parts: strategy-dependent compound severity (\\S\\ref{sec:strategy-severity}), phase-modulated amplification (\\S\\ref{sec:phase-amplification}), the phase-strategy crossover (\\S\\ref{sec:crossover}), cross-model replication (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}), and statistical validation (\\S\\ref{sec:stat-validation}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Overview}\n\\label{sec:exp-overview}\n\nWe evaluate compound sparsity on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B using a 50-problem stratified subset of MATH-500 (\\S\\ref{sec:dataset}). For each problem, we measure perplexity under four conditions---dense, attention-only pruning, KV-only eviction, and compound (both)---at sparsity levels $s \\in \\{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\\}$. We compare importance-based and random eviction strategies (\\S\\ref{sec:sparsity-config}), and segment each generated sequence into high-, mid-, and low-sparsity reasoning phases via quantile-based attention sparsity partitioning (\\S\\ref{sec:phase-seg}). The interaction index (II, Eq.~\\ref{eq:ii}) quantifies compound super-additivity, and the severity gradient ($G$, Eq.~\\ref{eq:gradient}) captures phase-dependent amplification.\nModels are evaluated in \\texttt{bfloat16} precision (\\S\\ref{sec:experimental-setup}). Full experimental details appear therein.\n\n\n\\subsection{Strategy-Dependent Severity (C1)}\n\\label{sec:strategy-severity}\n\nThe compound severity difference reveals a stark dependence on eviction strategy. Table~\\ref{tab:aggregate-ii} reports the median compound severity difference $\\Delta = \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}} - \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$ across 50 problems for each strategy and sparsity level. Under importance-based eviction, compound sparsity produces substantial super-additive degradation that grows with sparsity: $\\Delta$ increases from $0.39$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $14.59$ at $s{=}0.30$, a $37{\\times}$ amplification. Under random eviction, the severity difference shows no systematic growth, remaining near zero ($-0.02$ to $1.54$). % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030, bf16_ds_rand_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Median compound severity difference ($\\Delta = \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}} - \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$) by eviction strategy and sparsity level on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B ($n{=}50$ MATH-500 problems).}\n\\label{tab:aggregate-ii}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nStrategy & $s{=}0.15$ & $s{=}0.20$ & $s{=}0.25$ & $s{=}0.30$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nImportance & 0.39 & 1.48 & 7.16 & 14.59 \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json median_diff\nRandom     & 0.01 & $-$0.02 & 0.54 & 1.54 \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_rand_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json median_diff\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThis strategy dependence indicates that compound sparsity severity is not an intrinsic property of the compression level. Rather, the same sparsity configuration produces qualitatively different compound behavior depending on the eviction strategy---an emergent phase-strategy interaction whose structure we unpack in the following subsections.\n\n\n\\subsection{Phase-Modulated Amplification (C2)}\n\\label{sec:phase-amplification}\n\nDecomposing the interaction index by reasoning phase reveals that the strategy-dependent severity observed in \\S\\ref{sec:strategy-severity} is driven by amplification concentrated in the low-sparsity phase. Table~\\ref{tab:main-results} reports per-phase median II and the severity gradient $G$ at each sparsity level.\n\nUnder importance-based eviction, the severity gradient---the ratio of low-phase to high-phase median II---increases with sparsity from $69{\\times}$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $274{\\times}$ at $s{=}0.30$. The low-sparsity phase---corresponding to exploratory reasoning with broadly distributed attention---bears disproportionate compound degradation, while the high-sparsity phase---corresponding to structured computation with focused attention---experiences near-zero compound interaction ($\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$ ranges from $0.006$ to $0.053$). Under random eviction, the gradient remains near unity ($G \\approx 0.9$--$1.7{\\times}$) at all sparsity levels, confirming that phase modulation is specific to the emergent interaction between head pruning and importance-based scoring. % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030, bf16_ds_rand_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Interaction index (II) by eviction strategy, reasoning phase, and sparsity level. II values are problem-level medians across 50 problems. $\\Delta = \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}} - \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$ is the median severity difference. $G = \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}} / \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$ quantifies phase-dependent amplification.}\n\\label{tab:main-results}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llccccr@{}}\n\\toprule\n$s$ & Strategy & $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$ & $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{mid}}$ & $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}}$ & $\\Delta$ & $G$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.15 & Importance & 0.006 & 0.081 & 0.398 & 0.39 & $\\mathbf{69}$ \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015\n0.15 & Random     & 0.093 & 0.097 & 0.103 & 0.01 & 1.1 \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_rand_s015\n\\addlinespace\n0.20 & Importance & 0.015 & 0.203 & 1.496 & 1.48 & $\\mathbf{103}$ \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_importance_s020\n0.20 & Random     & 0.292 & 0.350 & 0.276 & $-$0.02 & 0.9 \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_rand_s020\n\\addlinespace\n0.25 & Importance & 0.032 & 1.118 & 7.196 & 7.16 & $\\mathbf{222}$ \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_importance_s025\n0.25 & Random     & 0.818 & 0.932 & 1.363 & 0.54 & 1.7 \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_rand_s025\n\\addlinespace\n0.30 & Importance & 0.053 & 2.283 & 14.639 & 14.59 & $\\mathbf{274}$ \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_importance_s030\n0.30 & Random     & 2.503 & 2.592 & 4.041 & 1.54 & 1.6 \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_rand_s030\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe gradient growth is sub-exponential: successive ratios of the median severity difference $\\Delta$ between adjacent sparsity levels are $3.8{\\times}$, $4.8{\\times}$, and $2.0{\\times}$---a decreasing terminal growth rate consistent with a ceiling effect as compound degradation in the low-sparsity phase approaches saturation. The severity gradient $G$ exhibits the same pattern, with consecutive ratios of $1.5{\\times}$, $2.2{\\times}$, and $1.2{\\times}$. Figure~\\ref{fig:hero} visualizes this trajectory, contrasting the steep importance-based curve against the flat random baseline. % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json median_diff\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Severity gradient ($G$) as a function of sparsity level $s$. Importance-based eviction (solid) exhibits an increasing gradient from $69{\\times}$ to $274{\\times}$, while random eviction (dashed) remains flat near $1{\\times}$. Shaded regions denote 95\\% bootstrap confidence intervals.} % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030, bf16_ds_rand_s015..s030\n\\label{fig:hero}\n\\end{figure}\n\nWe report medians throughout because the II distribution is right-skewed: the mean severity difference exceeds the median by a factor of $1.6{:}1$ at $s{=}0.30$ under importance-based eviction (mean $\\Delta = 22.94$ vs.\\ median $\\Delta = 14.59$). The median resists the influence of outlier problems with exceptionally long reasoning traces, while bootstrap confidence intervals (\\S\\ref{sec:stat-validation}) account for this skewness via the resampling distribution. % exp_id: bf16_importance_s030; source: cohens_d_bf16.json mean_diff=22.939, experiment_summary_table.json median_diff=14.586\n\n\n\\subsection{Phase-Strategy Crossover (C3)}\n\\label{sec:crossover}\n\nThe phase-modulated gradient produces a striking qualitative phenomenon: a \\emph{crossover} in which importance-based eviction is simultaneously the better and worse strategy depending on the reasoning phase. At $s{=}0.25$, importance-based eviction yields a lower II than random in the high-sparsity phase ($\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}} = 0.032$ vs.\\ $0.818$), but a higher II in the low-sparsity phase ($\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}} = 7.196$ vs.\\ $1.363$). This is not merely a magnitude difference---it is a reversal in the relative ranking of strategies across phases. % exp_id: bf16_importance_s025, bf16_ds_rand_s025; source: experiment_summary_table.json\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:crossover} displays the crossover structure as a heatmap of per-phase II values across strategies and sparsity levels.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_crossover.pdf}\n\\caption{Strategy-dependent crossover at $s{=}0.25$. Grouped bar chart of median II by eviction strategy and reasoning phase. Importance-based eviction outperforms random in the high-sparsity phase but produces $5.3{\\times}$ higher II in the low-sparsity phase, reversing the strategy ranking.} % exp_id: bf16_importance_s025, bf16_ds_rand_s025; 7.196/1.363=5.28\n\\label{fig:crossover}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe crossover emerges from an emergent phase-strategy interaction: in the high-sparsity phase---where attention is already focused---importance-based eviction preserves genuinely critical positions, yielding lower compound severity than random eviction. In the low-sparsity phase, however, head pruning redistributes attention mass across surviving heads, altering the importance score landscape in a way that couples the two sparsity mechanisms (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}). The result is that targeted eviction inadvertently removes positions that would have been retained under the pre-pruning importance ranking---producing \\emph{higher} compound severity than the explicitly random baseline.\n\nThe crossover is present at all four tested sparsity levels ($s \\geq 0.15$): at each level, importance-based eviction achieves lower II in the high-sparsity phase but higher II in the low-sparsity phase relative to random eviction. The magnitude of the low-phase reversal grows from $3.9{\\times}$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $5.4{\\times}$ at $s{=}0.20$, before moderating to $3.6{\\times}$ at $s{=}0.30$ as random eviction severity also rises. % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030, bf16_ds_rand_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json; ratios: 0.398/0.103=3.87, 1.496/0.276=5.42, 7.196/1.363=5.28, 14.639/4.041=3.62\n\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Replication (C5)}\n\\label{sec:cross-model}\n\nTo assess whether the phase-modulated severity gradient generalizes beyond DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B, we replicate the importance-based eviction experiments on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct. Both models share the Qwen2ForCausalLM backbone architecture (28 layers, 28 heads, 4-group GQA) but differ in training: DeepSeek uses reasoning distillation from DeepSeek-R1, while Qwen2.5 uses standard instruction tuning without explicit reasoning delimiters. This controlled comparison isolates the effect of training methodology on the compound sparsity interaction.\n\nDespite lacking explicit \\texttt{&lt;think>} delimiters, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct exhibits sufficient variation in attention sparsity across its generation trajectory to permit phase segmentation via the same quantile-based method (\\S\\ref{sec:phase-seg}). Four of the 50 problems are excluded due to degenerate phase assignments, yielding $n{=}46$ valid problems for Qwen (vs.\\ $n{=}50$ for DeepSeek).\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross-model} compares the median severity difference across both models.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-model replication: median compound severity difference ($\\Delta = \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}} - \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$) under importance-based eviction. Both models share the Qwen2 architecture but differ in training methodology (distillation vs.\\ instruction tuning).}\n\\label{tab:cross-model}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & $s{=}0.15$ & $s{=}0.20$ & $s{=}0.25$ & $s{=}0.30$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nDeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B & 0.39 & 1.48 & 7.16 & 14.59 \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json\nQwen2.5-7B-Instruct         & 0.04 & 0.23 & 1.27 & 2.94 \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_qw_imp_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nQwen2.5-7B-Instruct qualitatively reproduces the monotonic severity gradient observed in DeepSeek. The median severity difference increases from $0.04$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $2.94$ at $s{=}0.30$ (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model}), paralleling DeepSeek's $0.39$-to-$14.59$ trajectory at approximately one-tenth to one-fifth the absolute magnitude. Both models yield strictly increasing Cohen's $d$ trajectories (DeepSeek: $0.61 \\to 1.13$; Qwen: $0.37 \\to 1.44$; Table~\\ref{tab:cohens-d}), with Spearman $\\rho = 1.0$ between the four-point $d$ sequences (gradient permutation $p = 0.043$). The per-problem concordance across models is not significant (mean $\\rho = -0.11$, $p = 0.926$), indicating that while the aggregate gradient direction replicates, the specific problems driving severity differ between models---consistent with model-specific attention patterns rather than a universal per-problem vulnerability ranking. The Qwen effect is marginal at $s{=}0.15$ ($d{=}0.37$, bootstrap CI includes zero), becoming reliable at $s \\geq 0.20$ ($d \\geq 0.72$); this onset lag relative to DeepSeek is consistent with the lower overall compound severity in instruction-tuned models lacking explicit reasoning structure. % exp_id: cross_model_analysis.json (spearman_rho=1.0, gradient_permutation_p=0.043, per_problem_concordance_p=0.926, mean_rho=-0.107); cohens_d_bf16_qwen.json\n\nAs a control, random eviction on Qwen yields no systematic phase gradient at three of four sparsity levels (3/4 FAIL), mirroring DeepSeek's 4/4 FAIL pattern. At $s{=}0.30$, Qwen random produces a borderline PASS (permutation $p = 0.012$) but with negligible effect size (Cohen's $d = -0.04$, CI spans zero), indicating no meaningful phase modulation. % exp_id: bf16_qw_rand_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig5_crossmodel.pdf}\n\\caption{Cross-model comparison of the median severity difference under importance-based eviction. Both DeepSeek (solid) and Qwen (dashed) exhibit monotonically increasing $\\Delta$ with sparsity; Qwen's magnitude is approximately one-fifth of DeepSeek's.} % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030, bf16_qw_imp_s015..s030\n\\label{fig:cross-model}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe replication on a second model with distinct training methodology suggests that the phase-modulated severity gradient is not an artifact of DeepSeek's distillation-induced phase structure, but rather an emergent property of how compound sparsity interacts with attention dynamics in Transformer-based reasoning. We note that both models share the same backbone architecture; generalization to other architectures (e.g., LLaMA, Mistral) remains an open question (\\S\\ref{sec:limitations}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Validation}\n\\label{sec:stat-validation}\n\nWe validate the strategy-dependent severity using two complementary approaches: a paired permutation test on the difference statistic for significance, and Cohen's $d$ for effect size.\n\n\\paragraph{Permutation test.}\nFor each problem $i$ and sparsity level $s$, we compute the compound severity difference $\\Delta_i^{(\\text{strat})} = \\mathrm{II}_{\\textit{low},i} - \\mathrm{II}_{\\textit{high},i}$ under each eviction strategy. The test statistic is the median of the paired difference $\\widetilde{\\Delta} = \\text{median}_i\\!\\bigl(\\Delta_i^{(\\text{imp})} - \\Delta_i^{(\\text{rand})}\\bigr)$, measuring whether importance-based eviction produces systematically larger phase-dependent severity than random eviction. We permute strategy labels $10{,}000$ times to construct the null distribution and apply Bonferroni correction for 4 sparsity-level comparisons.\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:permutation} reports the results. All four sparsity levels reject $H_0$ (no strategy difference) after Bonferroni correction ($p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 0.005$), with observed median differences increasing monotonically from $0.37$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $9.85$ at $s{=}0.30$. % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest; source: permutation_test_results.json\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Paired permutation test (difference statistic) for compound severity: importance vs.\\ random eviction on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B ($n{=}49$ paired problems, $10{,}000$ permutations). $p$-values are Bonferroni-corrected for 4 comparisons.}\n\\label{tab:permutation}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}rrcl@{}}\n\\toprule\n$s$ & Observed $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$ & 95\\% CI & $p_{\\text{Bonf}}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.15 & 0.37 & $[0.19,\\; 0.62]$ & 0.0032 \\\\ % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest_s015; source: permutation_test_results.json s015.permutation_test_difference\n0.20 & 0.97 & $[0.69,\\; 1.49]$ & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\ % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest_s020; source: permutation_test_results.json s020.permutation_test_difference\n0.25 & 3.34 & $[1.66,\\; 6.64]$ & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\ % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest_s025; source: permutation_test_results.json s025.permutation_test_difference\n0.30 & 9.85 & $[6.70,\\; 16.29]$ & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\ % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest_s030; source: permutation_test_results.json s030.permutation_test_difference\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe monotonic growth of both the point estimate and the CI lower bound ($0.19 \\to 0.69 \\to 1.66 \\to 6.70$) confirms that compound severity amplification under importance-based eviction strengthens with sparsity---not merely in central tendency but also in worst-case lower bounds. % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest; source: permutation_test_results.json\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig4_bootstrap.pdf}\n\\caption{Forest plot of permutation test results for the difference statistic. Horizontal bars show 95\\% permutation CIs; diamonds mark observed median differences $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$. All CIs lie entirely above zero (vertical dashed line), confirming significant strategy-dependent severity at every sparsity level.}\n\\label{fig:permtest-forest}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Effect sizes.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:cohens-d} reports Cohen's $d$ (bootstrap $B{=}10{,}000$) measuring the standardized difference in II between low- and high-sparsity phases under importance-based eviction.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cohen's $d$ and per-problem median difference $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$ for the phase-dependent II (low $-$ high) under importance-based eviction. Bootstrap 95\\% CIs for $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$ ($B{=}10{,}000$, per-problem paired). DeepSeek: $n{=}49$ problems (1 excluded); Qwen: $n{=}46$ problems (4 excluded).}\n\\label{tab:cohens-d}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n$s$ & DeepSeek $d$ & $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$ [95\\% CI] & Qwen $d$ & $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$ [95\\% CI] \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.15 & 0.61 (medium) & 0.32\\; $[0.20,\\; 0.47]$ & 0.37 (small) & 0.05\\; $[-0.01,\\; 0.11]$\\textsuperscript{ns} \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_imp_s015, bf16_qw_imp_s015; per-problem paired B=10000\n0.20 & 0.73 (medium) & 1.15\\; $[0.66,\\; 1.26]$ & 0.72 (medium) & 0.21\\; $[0.07,\\; 0.40]$ \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_imp_s020, bf16_qw_imp_s020; per-problem paired B=10000\n0.25 & 0.95 (large) & 3.97\\; $[2.83,\\; 5.29]$ & 1.23 (large) & 1.11\\; $[0.61,\\; 1.47]$ \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_imp_s025, bf16_qw_imp_s025; per-problem paired B=10000\n0.30 & 1.13 (large) & 9.94\\; $[8.39,\\; 16.65]$ & 1.44 (large) & 2.62\\; $[1.72,\\; 3.65]$ \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_imp_s030, bf16_qw_imp_s030;     "originalFile": "\n\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\nWe present our empirical findings in five parts: strategy-dependent compound severity (\\S\\ref{sec:strategy-severity}), phase-modulated amplification (\\S\\ref{sec:phase-amplification}), the phase-strategy crossover (\\S\\ref{sec:crossover}), cross-model replication (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}), and statistical validation (\\S\\ref{sec:stat-validation}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Overview}\n\\label{sec:exp-overview}\n\nWe evaluate compound sparsity on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B using a 50-problem stratified subset of MATH-500 (\\S\\ref{sec:dataset}). For each problem, we measure perplexity under four conditions---dense, attention-only pruning, KV-only eviction, and compound (both)---at sparsity levels $s \\in \\{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\\}$. We compare importance-based and random eviction strategies (\\S\\ref{sec:sparsity-config}), and segment each generated sequence into high-, mid-, and low-sparsity reasoning phases via quantile-based attention sparsity partitioning (\\S\\ref{sec:phase-seg}). The interaction index (II, Eq.~\\ref{eq:ii}) quantifies compound super-additivity, and the severity gradient ($G$, Eq.~\\ref{eq:gradient}) captures phase-dependent amplification.\nModels are evaluated in \\texttt{bfloat16} precision (\\S\\ref{sec:experimental-setup}). Full experimental details appear therein.\n\n\n\\subsection{Strategy-Dependent Severity (C1)}\n\\label{sec:strategy-severity}\n\nThe compound severity difference reveals a stark dependence on eviction strategy. Table~\\ref{tab:aggregate-ii} reports the median compound severity difference $\\Delta = \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}} - \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$ across 50 problems for each strategy and sparsity level. Under importance-based eviction, compound sparsity produces substantial super-additive degradation that grows with sparsity: $\\Delta$ increases from $0.39$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $14.59$ at $s{=}0.30$, a $37{\\times}$ amplification. Under random eviction, the severity difference shows no systematic growth, remaining near zero ($-0.02$ to $1.54$). % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030, bf16_ds_rand_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Median compound severity difference ($\\Delta = \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}} - \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$) by eviction strategy and sparsity level on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B ($n{=}50$ MATH-500 problems).}\n\\label{tab:aggregate-ii}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nStrategy & $s{=}0.15$ & $s{=}0.20$ & $s{=}0.25$ & $s{=}0.30$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nImportance & 0.39 & 1.48 & 7.16 & 14.59 \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json median_diff\nRandom     & 0.01 & $-$0.02 & 0.54 & 1.54 \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_rand_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json median_diff\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThis strategy dependence indicates that compound sparsity severity is not an intrinsic property of the compression level. Rather, the same sparsity configuration produces qualitatively different compound behavior depending on the eviction strategy---an emergent phase-strategy interaction whose structure we unpack in the following subsections.\n\n\n\\subsection{Phase-Modulated Amplification (C2)}\n\\label{sec:phase-amplification}\n\nDecomposing the interaction index by reasoning phase reveals that the strategy-dependent severity observed in \\S\\ref{sec:strategy-severity} is driven by amplification concentrated in the low-sparsity phase. Table~\\ref{tab:main-results} reports per-phase median II and the severity gradient $G$ at each sparsity level.\n\nUnder importance-based eviction, the severity gradient---the ratio of low-phase to high-phase median II---increases with sparsity from $69{\\times}$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $274{\\times}$ at $s{=}0.30$. The low-sparsity phase---corresponding to exploratory reasoning with broadly distributed attention---bears disproportionate compound degradation, while the high-sparsity phase---corresponding to structured computation with focused attention---experiences near-zero compound interaction ($\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$ ranges from $0.006$ to $0.053$). Under random eviction, the gradient remains near unity ($G \\approx 0.9$--$1.7{\\times}$) at all sparsity levels, confirming that phase modulation is specific to the emergent interaction between head pruning and importance-based scoring. % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030, bf16_ds_rand_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Interaction index (II) by eviction strategy, reasoning phase, and sparsity level. II values are problem-level medians across 50 problems. $\\Delta = \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}} - \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$ is the median severity difference. $G = \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}} / \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$ quantifies phase-dependent amplification.}\n\\label{tab:main-results}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llccccr@{}}\n\\toprule\n$s$ & Strategy & $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$ & $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{mid}}$ & $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}}$ & $\\Delta$ & $G$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.15 & Importance & 0.006 & 0.081 & 0.398 & 0.39 & $\\mathbf{69}$ \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015\n0.15 & Random     & 0.093 & 0.097 & 0.103 & 0.01 & 1.1 \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_rand_s015\n\\addlinespace\n0.20 & Importance & 0.015 & 0.203 & 1.496 & 1.48 & $\\mathbf{103}$ \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_importance_s020\n0.20 & Random     & 0.292 & 0.350 & 0.276 & $-$0.02 & 0.9 \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_rand_s020\n\\addlinespace\n0.25 & Importance & 0.032 & 1.118 & 7.196 & 7.16 & $\\mathbf{222}$ \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_importance_s025\n0.25 & Random     & 0.818 & 0.932 & 1.363 & 0.54 & 1.7 \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_rand_s025\n\\addlinespace\n0.30 & Importance & 0.053 & 2.283 & 14.639 & 14.59 & $\\mathbf{274}$ \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_importance_s030\n0.30 & Random     & 2.503 & 2.592 & 4.041 & 1.54 & 1.6 \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_rand_s030\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe gradient growth is sub-exponential: successive ratios of the median severity difference $\\Delta$ between adjacent sparsity levels are $3.8{\\times}$, $4.8{\\times}$, and $2.0{\\times}$---a decreasing terminal growth rate consistent with a ceiling effect as compound degradation in the low-sparsity phase approaches saturation. The severity gradient $G$ exhibits the same pattern, with consecutive ratios of $1.5{\\times}$, $2.2{\\times}$, and $1.2{\\times}$. Figure~\\ref{fig:hero} visualizes this trajectory, contrasting the steep importance-based curve against the flat random baseline. % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json median_diff\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Severity gradient ($G$) as a function of sparsity level $s$. Importance-based eviction (solid) exhibits an increasing gradient from $69{\\times}$ to $274{\\times}$, while random eviction (dashed) remains flat near $1{\\times}$. Shaded regions denote 95\\% bootstrap confidence intervals.} % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030, bf16_ds_rand_s015..s030\n\\label{fig:hero}\n\\end{figure}\n\nWe report medians throughout because the II distribution is right-skewed: the mean severity difference exceeds the median by a factor of $1.6{:}1$ at $s{=}0.30$ under importance-based eviction (mean $\\Delta = 22.94$ vs.\\ median $\\Delta = 14.59$). The median resists the influence of outlier problems with exceptionally long reasoning traces, while bootstrap confidence intervals (\\S\\ref{sec:stat-validation}) account for this skewness via the resampling distribution. % exp_id: bf16_importance_s030; source: cohens_d_bf16.json mean_diff=22.939, experiment_summary_table.json median_diff=14.586\n\n\n\\subsection{Phase-Strategy Crossover (C3)}\n\\label{sec:crossover}\n\nThe phase-modulated gradient produces a striking qualitative phenomenon: a \\emph{crossover} in which importance-based eviction is simultaneously the better and worse strategy depending on the reasoning phase. At $s{=}0.25$, importance-based eviction yields a lower II than random in the high-sparsity phase ($\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}} = 0.032$ vs.\\ $0.818$), but a higher II in the low-sparsity phase ($\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}} = 7.196$ vs.\\ $1.363$). This is not merely a magnitude difference---it is a reversal in the relative ranking of strategies across phases. % exp_id: bf16_importance_s025, bf16_ds_rand_s025; source: experiment_summary_table.json\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:crossover} displays the crossover structure as a heatmap of per-phase II values across strategies and sparsity levels.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_crossover.pdf}\n\\caption{Strategy-dependent crossover at $s{=}0.25$. Grouped bar chart of median II by eviction strategy and reasoning phase. Importance-based eviction outperforms random in the high-sparsity phase but produces $5.3{\\times}$ higher II in the low-sparsity phase, reversing the strategy ranking.} % exp_id: bf16_importance_s025, bf16_ds_rand_s025; 7.196/1.363=5.28\n\\label{fig:crossover}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe crossover emerges from an emergent phase-strategy interaction: in the high-sparsity phase---where attention is already focused---importance-based eviction preserves genuinely critical positions, yielding lower compound severity than random eviction. In the low-sparsity phase, however, head pruning redistributes attention mass across surviving heads, altering the importance score landscape in a way that couples the two sparsity mechanisms (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}). The result is that targeted eviction inadvertently removes positions that would have been retained under the pre-pruning importance ranking---producing \\emph{higher} compound severity than the explicitly random baseline.\n\nThe crossover is present at all four tested sparsity levels ($s \\geq 0.15$): at each level, importance-based eviction achieves lower II in the high-sparsity phase but higher II in the low-sparsity phase relative to random eviction. The magnitude of the low-phase reversal grows from $3.9{\\times}$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $5.4{\\times}$ at $s{=}0.20$, before moderating to $3.6{\\times}$ at $s{=}0.30$ as random eviction severity also rises. % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030, bf16_ds_rand_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json; ratios: 0.398/0.103=3.87, 1.496/0.276=5.42, 7.196/1.363=5.28, 14.639/4.041=3.62\n\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Replication (C5)}\n\\label{sec:cross-model}\n\nTo assess whether the phase-modulated severity gradient generalizes beyond DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B, we replicate the importance-based eviction experiments on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct. Both models share the Qwen2ForCausalLM backbone architecture (28 layers, 28 heads, 4-group GQA) but differ in training: DeepSeek uses reasoning distillation from DeepSeek-R1, while Qwen2.5 uses standard instruction tuning without explicit reasoning delimiters. This controlled comparison isolates the effect of training methodology on the compound sparsity interaction.\n\nDespite lacking explicit \\texttt{&lt;think>} delimiters, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct exhibits sufficient variation in attention sparsity across its generation trajectory to permit phase segmentation via the same quantile-based method (\\S\\ref{sec:phase-seg}). Four of the 50 problems are excluded due to degenerate phase assignments, yielding $n{=}46$ valid problems for Qwen (vs.\\ $n{=}50$ for DeepSeek).\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross-model} compares the median severity difference across both models.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-model replication: median compound severity difference ($\\Delta = \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}} - \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$) under importance-based eviction. Both models share the Qwen2 architecture but differ in training methodology (distillation vs.\\ instruction tuning).}\n\\label{tab:cross-model}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & $s{=}0.15$ & $s{=}0.20$ & $s{=}0.25$ & $s{=}0.30$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nDeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B & 0.39 & 1.48 & 7.16 & 14.59 \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json\nQwen2.5-7B-Instruct         & 0.04 & 0.23 & 1.27 & 2.94 \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_qw_imp_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nQwen2.5-7B-Instruct qualitatively reproduces the monotonic severity gradient observed in DeepSeek. The median severity difference increases from $0.04$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $2.94$ at $s{=}0.30$ (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model}), paralleling DeepSeek's $0.39$-to-$14.59$ trajectory at approximately one-tenth to one-fifth the absolute magnitude. Both models yield strictly increasing Cohen's $d$ trajectories (DeepSeek: $0.61 \\to 1.13$; Qwen: $0.37 \\to 1.44$; Table~\\ref{tab:cohens-d}), with Spearman $\\rho = 1.0$ between the four-point $d$ sequences (gradient permutation $p = 0.043$). The per-problem concordance across models is not significant (mean $\\rho = -0.11$, $p = 0.926$), indicating that while the aggregate gradient direction replicates, the specific problems driving severity differ between models---consistent with model-specific attention patterns rather than a universal per-problem vulnerability ranking. The Qwen effect is marginal at $s{=}0.15$ ($d{=}0.37$, bootstrap CI includes zero), becoming reliable at $s \\geq 0.20$ ($d \\geq 0.72$); this onset lag relative to DeepSeek is consistent with the lower overall compound severity in instruction-tuned models lacking explicit reasoning structure. % exp_id: cross_model_analysis.json (spearman_rho=1.0, gradient_permutation_p=0.043, per_problem_concordance_p=0.926, mean_rho=-0.107); cohens_d_bf16_qwen.json\n\nAs a control, random eviction on Qwen yields no systematic phase gradient at three of four sparsity levels (3/4 FAIL), mirroring DeepSeek's 4/4 FAIL pattern. At $s{=}0.30$, Qwen random produces a borderline PASS (permutation $p = 0.012$) but with negligible effect size (Cohen's $d = -0.04$, CI spans zero), indicating no meaningful phase modulation. % exp_id: bf16_qw_rand_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig5_crossmodel.pdf}\n\\caption{Cross-model comparison of the median severity difference under importance-based eviction. Both DeepSeek (solid) and Qwen (dashed) exhibit monotonically increasing $\\Delta$ with sparsity; Qwen's magnitude is approximately one-fifth of DeepSeek's.} % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030, bf16_qw_imp_s015..s030\n\\label{fig:cross-model}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe replication on a second model with distinct training methodology suggests that the phase-modulated severity gradient is not an artifact of DeepSeek's distillation-induced phase structure, but rather an emergent property of how compound sparsity interacts with attention dynamics in Transformer-based reasoning. We note that both models share the same backbone architecture; generalization to other architectures (e.g., LLaMA, Mistral) remains an open question (\\S\\ref{sec:limitations}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Validation}\n\\label{sec:stat-validation}\n\nWe validate the strategy-dependent severity using two complementary approaches: a paired permutation test on the difference statistic for significance, and Cohen's $d$ for effect size.\n\n\\paragraph{Permutation test.}\nFor each problem $i$ and sparsity level $s$, we compute the compound severity difference $\\Delta_i^{(\\text{strat})} = \\mathrm{II}_{\\textit{low},i} - \\mathrm{II}_{\\textit{high},i}$ under each eviction strategy. The test statistic is the median of the paired difference $\\widetilde{\\Delta} = \\text{median}_i\\!\\bigl(\\Delta_i^{(\\text{imp})} - \\Delta_i^{(\\text{rand})}\\bigr)$, measuring whether importance-based eviction produces systematically larger phase-dependent severity than random eviction. We permute strategy labels $10{,}000$ times to construct the null distribution and apply Bonferroni correction for 4 sparsity-level comparisons.\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:permutation} reports the results. All four sparsity levels reject $H_0$ (no strategy difference) after Bonferroni correction ($p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 0.005$), with observed median differences increasing monotonically from $0.37$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $9.85$ at $s{=}0.30$. % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest; source: permutation_test_results.json\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Paired permutation test (difference statistic) for compound severity: importance vs.\\ random eviction on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B ($n{=}49$ paired problems, $10{,}000$ permutations). $p$-values are Bonferroni-corrected for 4 comparisons.}\n\\label{tab:permutation}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}rrcl@{}}\n\\toprule\n$s$ & Observed $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$ & 95\\% CI & $p_{\\text{Bonf}}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.15 & 0.37 & $[0.19,\\; 0.62]$ & 0.0032 \\\\ % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest_s015; source: permutation_test_results.json s015.permutation_test_difference\n0.20 & 0.97 & $[0.69,\\; 1.49]$ & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\ % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest_s020; source: permutation_test_results.json s020.permutation_test_difference\n0.25 & 3.34 & $[1.66,\\; 6.64]$ & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\ % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest_s025; source: permutation_test_results.json s025.permutation_test_difference\n0.30 & 9.85 & $[6.70,\\; 16.29]$ & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\ % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest_s030; source: permutation_test_results.json s030.permutation_test_difference\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe monotonic growth of both the point estimate and the CI lower bound ($0.19 \\to 0.69 \\to 1.66 \\to 6.70$) confirms that compound severity amplification under importance-based eviction strengthens with sparsity---not merely in central tendency but also in worst-case lower bounds. % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest; source: permutation_test_results.json\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig4_bootstrap.pdf}\n\\caption{Forest plot of permutation test results for the difference statistic. Horizontal bars show 95\\% permutation CIs; diamonds mark observed median differences $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$. All CIs lie entirely above zero (vertical dashed line), confirming significant strategy-dependent severity at every sparsity level.}\n\\label{fig:permtest-forest}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Effect sizes.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:cohens-d} reports Cohen's $d$ (bootstrap $B{=}10{,}000$) measuring the standardized difference in II between low- and high-sparsity phases under importance-based eviction.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cohen's $d$ and per-problem median difference $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$ for the phase-dependent II (low $-$ high) under importance-based eviction. Bootstrap 95\\% CIs for $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$ ($B{=}10{,}000$, per-problem paired). DeepSeek: $n{=}49$ problems (1 excluded); Qwen: $n{=}46$ problems (4 excluded).}\n\\label{tab:cohens-d}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n$s$ & DeepSeek $d$ & $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$ [95\\% CI] & Qwen $d$ & $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$ [95\\% CI] \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.15 & 0.61 (medium) & 0.32\\; $[0.20,\\; 0.47]$ & 0.37 (small) & 0.05\\; $[-0.01,\\; 0.11]$\\textsuperscript{ns} \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_imp_s015, bf16_qw_imp_s015; per-problem paired B=10000\n0.20 & 0.73 (medium) & 1.15\\; $[0.66,\\; 1.26]$ & 0.72 (medium) & 0.21\\; $[0.07,\\; 0.40]$ \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_imp_s020, bf16_qw_imp_s020; per-problem paired B=10000\n0.25 & 0.95 (large) & 3.97\\; $[2.83,\\; 5.29]$ & 1.23 (large) & 1.11\\; $[0.61,\\; 1.47]$ \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_imp_s025, bf16_qw_imp_s025; per-problem paired B=10000\n0.30 & 1.13 (large) & 9.94\\; $[8.39,\\; 16.65]$ & 1.44 (large) & 2.62\\; $[1.72,\\; 3.65]$ \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_imp_s030, bf16_qw_imp_s030; per-problem paired B=10000\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{5}{@{}l}{\\footnotesize \\textsuperscript{ns}CI spans zero; marginal at $s{=}0.15$ (bootstrap CI includes zero), becoming reliable at $s \\geq 0.20$.}\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nFor DeepSeek, the effect size increases monotonically from medium ($d{=}0.61$) at $s{=}0.15$ to large ($d{=}1.13$) at $s{=}0.30$, with all bootstrap CIs for $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$ excluding zero (Table~\\ref{tab:cohens-d}). % exp_id: bf16_ds_imp_s015..s030; per-problem paired B=10000\nFor Qwen, the compound interaction effect emerges at $s \\geq 0.20$ ($d > 0.5$), with the $s{=}0.15$ CI spanning zero ($d{=}0.28$, $[-0.01, 0.60]$), suggesting a model-dependent onset threshold for detectable phase modulation. % exp_id: cohens_d_bf16_qwen.json s015\nAt higher sparsity levels ($s \\geq 0.25$), Qwen exhibits larger standardized effect sizes than DeepSeek ($d{=}0.89$/$1.02$ vs.\\ $0.68$/$0.80$), consistent with lower between-problem variance in Qwen's II distribution yielding larger standardized differences despite smaller absolute II values. % exp_id: cohens_d_bf16.json, cohens_d_bf16_qwen.json\nFull per-problem distributions and permutation null distributions appear in Appendix~\\ref{app:bootstrap-details}.\n",
```

```json
    "originalFile": "\n\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\nWe present our empirical findings in five parts: strategy-dependent compound severity (\\S\\ref{sec:strategy-severity}), phase-modulated amplification (\\S\\ref{sec:phase-amplification}), the phase-strategy crossover (\\S\\ref{sec:crossover}), cross-model replication (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}), and statistical validation (\\S\\ref{sec:stat-validation}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Overview}\n\\label{sec:exp-overview}\n\nWe evaluate compound sparsity on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B using a 50-problem stratified subset of MATH-500 (\\S\\ref{sec:dataset}). For each problem, we measure perplexity under four conditions---dense, attention-only pruning, KV-only eviction, and compound (both)---at sparsity levels $s \\in \\{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\\}$. We compare importance-based and random eviction strategies (\\S\\ref{sec:sparsity-config}), and segment each generated sequence into high-, mid-, and low-sparsity reasoning phases via quantile-based attention sparsity partitioning (\\S\\ref{sec:phase-seg}). The interaction index (II, Eq.~\\ref{eq:ii}) quantifies compound super-additivity, and the severity gradient ($G$, Eq.~\\ref{eq:gradient}) captures phase-dependent amplification.\nModels are evaluated in \\texttt{bfloat16} precision (\\S\\ref{sec:experimental-setup}). Full experimental details appear therein.\n\n\n\\subsection{Strategy-Dependent Severity (C1)}\n\\label{sec:strategy-severity}\n\nThe compound severity difference reveals a stark dependence on eviction strategy. Table~\\ref{tab:aggregate-ii} reports the median compound severity difference $\\Delta = \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}} - \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$ across 50 problems for each strategy and sparsity level. Under importance-based eviction, compound sparsity produces substantial super-additive degradation that grows with sparsity: $\\Delta$ increases from $0.39$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $14.59$ at $s{=}0.30$, a $37{\\times}$ amplification. Under random eviction, the severity difference shows no systematic growth, remaining near zero ($-0.02$ to $1.54$). % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030, bf16_ds_rand_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Median compound severity difference ($\\Delta = \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}} - \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$) by eviction strategy and sparsity level on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B ($n{=}50$ MATH-500 problems).}\n\\label{tab:aggregate-ii}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nStrategy & $s{=}0.15$ & $s{=}0.20$ & $s{=}0.25$ & $s{=}0.30$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nImportance & 0.39 & 1.48 & 7.16 & 14.59 \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json median_diff\nRandom     & 0.01 & $-$0.02 & 0.54 & 1.54 \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_rand_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json median_diff\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThis strategy dependence indicates that compound sparsity severity is not an intrinsic property of the compression level. Rather, the same sparsity configuration produces qualitatively different compound behavior depending on the eviction strategy---an emergent phase-strategy interaction whose structure we unpack in the following subsections.\n\n\n\\subsection{Phase-Modulated Amplification (C2)}\n\\label{sec:phase-amplification}\n\nDecomposing the interaction index by reasoning phase reveals that the strategy-dependent severity observed in \\S\\ref{sec:strategy-severity} is driven by amplification concentrated in the low-sparsity phase. Table~\\ref{tab:main-results} reports per-phase median II and the severity gradient $G$ at each sparsity level.\n\nUnder importance-based eviction, the severity gradient---the ratio of low-phase to high-phase median II---increases with sparsity from $69{\\times}$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $274{\\times}$ at $s{=}0.30$. The low-sparsity phase---corresponding to exploratory reasoning with broadly distributed attention---bears disproportionate compound degradation, while the high-sparsity phase---corresponding to structured computation with focused attention---experiences near-zero compound interaction ($\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$ ranges from $0.006$ to $0.053$). Under random eviction, the gradient remains near unity ($G \\approx 0.9$--$1.7{\\times}$) at all sparsity levels, confirming that phase modulation is specific to the emergent interaction between head pruning and importance-based scoring. % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030, bf16_ds_rand_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Interaction index (II) by eviction strategy, reasoning phase, and sparsity level. II values are problem-level medians across 50 problems. $\\Delta = \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}} - \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$ is the median severity difference. $G = \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}} / \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$ quantifies phase-dependent amplification.}\n\\label{tab:main-results}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llccccr@{}}\n\\toprule\n$s$ & Strategy & $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$ & $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{mid}}$ & $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}}$ & $\\Delta$ & $G$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.15 & Importance & 0.006 & 0.081 & 0.398 & 0.39 & $\\mathbf{69}$ \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015\n0.15 & Random     & 0.093 & 0.097 & 0.103 & 0.01 & 1.1 \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_rand_s015\n\\addlinespace\n0.20 & Importance & 0.015 & 0.203 & 1.496 & 1.48 & $\\mathbf{103}$ \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_importance_s020\n0.20 & Random     & 0.292 & 0.350 & 0.276 & $-$0.02 & 0.9 \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_rand_s020\n\\addlinespace\n0.25 & Importance & 0.032 & 1.118 & 7.196 & 7.16 & $\\mathbf{222}$ \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_importance_s025\n0.25 & Random     & 0.818 & 0.932 & 1.363 & 0.54 & 1.7 \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_rand_s025\n\\addlinespace\n0.30 & Importance & 0.053 & 2.283 & 14.639 & 14.59 & $\\mathbf{274}$ \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_importance_s030\n0.30 & Random     & 2.503 & 2.592 & 4.041 & 1.54 & 1.6 \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_rand_s030\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe gradient growth is sub-exponential: successive ratios of the median severity difference $\\Delta$ between adjacent sparsity levels are $3.8{\\times}$, $4.8{\\times}$, and $2.0{\\times}$---a decreasing terminal growth rate consistent with a ceiling effect as compound degradation in the low-sparsity phase approaches saturation. The severity gradient $G$ exhibits the same pattern, with consecutive ratios of $1.5{\\times}$, $2.2{\\times}$, and $1.2{\\times}$. Figure~\\ref{fig:hero} visualizes this trajectory, contrasting the steep importance-based curve against the flat random baseline. % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json median_diff\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Severity gradient ($G$) as a function of sparsity level $s$. Importance-based eviction (solid) exhibits an increasing gradient from $69{\\times}$ to $274{\\times}$, while random eviction (dashed) remains flat near $1{\\times}$. Shaded regions denote 95\\% bootstrap confidence intervals.} % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030, bf16_ds_rand_s015..s030\n\\label{fig:hero}\n\\end{figure}\n\nWe report medians throughout because the II distribution is right-skewed: the mean severity difference exceeds the median by a factor of $1.6{:}1$ at $s{=}0.30$ under importance-based eviction (mean $\\Delta = 22.94$ vs.\\ median $\\Delta = 14.59$). The median resists the influence of outlier problems with exceptionally long reasoning traces, while bootstrap confidence intervals (\\S\\ref{sec:stat-validation}) account for this skewness via the resampling distribution. % exp_id: bf16_importance_s030; source: cohens_d_bf16.json mean_diff=22.939, experiment_summary_table.json median_diff=14.586\n\n\n\\subsection{Phase-Strategy Crossover (C3)}\n\\label{sec:crossover}\n\nThe phase-modulated gradient produces a striking qualitative phenomenon: a \\emph{crossover} in which importance-based eviction is simultaneously the better and worse strategy depending on the reasoning phase. At $s{=}0.25$, importance-based eviction yields a lower II than random in the high-sparsity phase ($\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}} = 0.032$ vs.\\ $0.818$), but a higher II in the low-sparsity phase ($\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}} = 7.196$ vs.\\ $1.363$). This is not merely a magnitude difference---it is a reversal in the relative ranking of strategies across phases. % exp_id: bf16_importance_s025, bf16_ds_rand_s025; source: experiment_summary_table.json\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:crossover} displays the crossover structure as a heatmap of per-phase II values across strategies and sparsity levels.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_crossover.pdf}\n\\caption{Strategy-dependent crossover at $s{=}0.25$. Grouped bar chart of median II by eviction strategy and reasoning phase. Importance-based eviction outperforms random in the high-sparsity phase but produces $5.3{\\times}$ higher II in the low-sparsity phase, reversing the strategy ranking.} % exp_id: bf16_importance_s025, bf16_ds_rand_s025; 7.196/1.363=5.28\n\\label{fig:crossover}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe crossover emerges from an emergent phase-strategy interaction: in the high-sparsity phase---where attention is already focused---importance-based eviction preserves genuinely critical positions, yielding lower compound severity than random eviction. In the low-sparsity phase, however, head pruning redistributes attention mass across surviving heads, altering the importance score landscape in a way that couples the two sparsity mechanisms (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}). The result is that targeted eviction inadvertently removes positions that would have been retained under the pre-pruning importance ranking---producing \\emph{higher} compound severity than the explicitly random baseline.\n\nThe crossover is present at all four tested sparsity levels ($s \\geq 0.15$): at each level, importance-based eviction achieves lower II in the high-sparsity phase but higher II in the low-sparsity phase relative to random eviction. The magnitude of the low-phase reversal grows from $3.9{\\times}$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $5.4{\\times}$ at $s{=}0.20$, before moderating to $3.6{\\times}$ at $s{=}0.30$ as random eviction severity also rises. % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030, bf16_ds_rand_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json; ratios: 0.398/0.103=3.87, 1.496/0.276=5.42, 7.196/1.363=5.28, 14.639/4.041=3.62\n\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Replication (C5)}\n\\label{sec:cross-model}\n\nTo assess whether the phase-modulated severity gradient generalizes beyond DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B, we replicate the importance-based eviction experiments on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct. Both models share the Qwen2ForCausalLM backbone architecture (28 layers, 28 heads, 4-group GQA) but differ in training: DeepSeek uses reasoning distillation from DeepSeek-R1, while Qwen2.5 uses standard instruction tuning without explicit reasoning delimiters. This controlled comparison isolates the effect of training methodology on the compound sparsity interaction.\n\nDespite lacking explicit \\texttt{&lt;think>} delimiters, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct exhibits sufficient variation in attention sparsity across its generation trajectory to permit phase segmentation via the same quantile-based method (\\S\\ref{sec:phase-seg}). Four of the 50 problems are excluded due to degenerate phase assignments, yielding $n{=}46$ valid problems for Qwen (vs.\\ $n{=}50$ for DeepSeek).\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross-model} compares the median severity difference across both models.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-model replication: median compound severity difference ($\\Delta = \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}} - \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$) under importance-based eviction. Both models share the Qwen2 architecture but differ in training methodology (distillation vs.\\ instruction tuning).}\n\\label{tab:cross-model}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & $s{=}0.15$ & $s{=}0.20$ & $s{=}0.25$ & $s{=}0.30$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nDeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B & 0.39 & 1.48 & 7.16 & 14.59 \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json\nQwen2.5-7B-Instruct         & 0.04 & 0.23 & 1.27 & 2.94 \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_qw_imp_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nQwen2.5-7B-Instruct qualitatively reproduces the monotonic severity gradient observed in DeepSeek. The median severity difference increases from $0.04$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $2.94$ at $s{=}0.30$ (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model}), paralleling DeepSeek's $0.39$-to-$14.59$ trajectory at approximately one-tenth to one-fifth the absolute magnitude. Both models yield strictly increasing Cohen's $d$ trajectories (DeepSeek: $0.61 \\to 1.13$; Qwen: $0.37 \\to 1.44$; Table~\\ref{tab:cohens-d}), with Spearman $\\rho = 1.0$ between the four-point $d$ sequences (gradient permutation $p = 0.043$). The per-problem concordance across models is not significant (mean $\\rho = -0.11$, $p = 0.926$), indicating that while the aggregate gradient direction replicates, the specific problems driving severity differ between models---consistent with model-specific attention patterns rather than a universal per-problem vulnerability ranking. The Qwen effect is marginal at $s{=}0.15$ ($d{=}0.37$, bootstrap CI includes zero), becoming reliable at $s \\geq 0.20$ ($d \\geq 0.72$); this onset lag relative to DeepSeek is consistent with the lower overall compound severity in instruction-tuned models lacking explicit reasoning structure. % exp_id: cross_model_analysis.json (spearman_rho=1.0, gradient_permutation_p=0.043, per_problem_concordance_p=0.926, mean_rho=-0.107); cohens_d_bf16_qwen.json\n\nAs a control, random eviction on Qwen yields no systematic phase gradient at three of four sparsity levels (3/4 FAIL), mirroring DeepSeek's 4/4 FAIL pattern. At $s{=}0.30$, Qwen random produces a borderline PASS (permutation $p = 0.012$) but with negligible effect size (Cohen's $d = -0.04$, CI spans zero), indicating no meaningful phase modulation. % exp_id: bf16_qw_rand_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig5_crossmodel.pdf}\n\\caption{Cross-model comparison of the median severity difference under importance-based eviction. Both DeepSeek (solid) and Qwen (dashed) exhibit monotonically increasing $\\Delta$ with sparsity; Qwen's magnitude is approximately one-fifth of DeepSeek's.} % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030, bf16_qw_imp_s015..s030\n\\label{fig:cross-model}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe replication on a second model with distinct training methodology suggests that the phase-modulated severity gradient is not an artifact of DeepSeek's distillation-induced phase structure, but rather an emergent property of how compound sparsity interacts with attention dynamics in Transformer-based reasoning. We note that both models share the same backbone architecture; generalization to other architectures (e.g., LLaMA, Mistral) remains an open question (\\S\\ref{sec:limitations}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Validation}\n\\label{sec:stat-validation}\n\nWe validate the strategy-dependent severity using two complementary approaches: a paired permutation test on the difference statistic for significance, and Cohen's $d$ for effect size.\n\n\\paragraph{Permutation test.}\nFor each problem $i$ and sparsity level $s$, we compute the compound severity difference $\\Delta_i^{(\\text{strat})} = \\mathrm{II}_{\\textit{low},i} - \\mathrm{II}_{\\textit{high},i}$ under each eviction strategy. The test statistic is the median of the paired difference $\\widetilde{\\Delta} = \\text{median}_i\\!\\bigl(\\Delta_i^{(\\text{imp})} - \\Delta_i^{(\\text{rand})}\\bigr)$, measuring whether importance-based eviction produces systematically larger phase-dependent severity than random eviction. We permute strategy labels $10{,}000$ times to construct the null distribution and apply Bonferroni correction for 4 sparsity-level comparisons.\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:permutation} reports the results. All four sparsity levels reject $H_0$ (no strategy difference) after Bonferroni correction ($p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 0.005$), with observed median differences increasing monotonically from $0.37$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $9.85$ at $s{=}0.30$. % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest; source: permutation_test_results.json\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Paired permutation test (difference statistic) for compound severity: importance vs.\\ random eviction on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B ($n{=}49$ paired problems, $10{,}000$ permutations). $p$-values are Bonferroni-corrected for 4 comparisons.}\n\\label{tab:permutation}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}rrcl@{}}\n\\toprule\n$s$ & Observed $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$ & 95\\% CI & $p_{\\text{Bonf}}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.15 & 0.37 & $[0.19,\\; 0.62]$ & 0.0032 \\\\ % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest_s015; source: permutation_test_results.json s015.permutation_test_difference\n0.20 & 0.97 & $[0.69,\\; 1.49]$ & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\ % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest_s020; source: permutation_test_results.json s020.permutation_test_difference\n0.25 & 3.34 & $[1.66,\\; 6.64]$ & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\ % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest_s025; source: permutation_test_results.json s025.permutation_test_difference\n0.30 & 9.85 & $[6.70,\\; 16.29]$ & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\ % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest_s030; source: permutation_test_results.json s030.permutation_test_difference\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe monotonic growth of both the point estimate and the CI lower bound ($0.19 \\to 0.69 \\to 1.66 \\to 6.70$) confirms that compound severity amplification under importance-based eviction strengthens with sparsity---not merely in central tendency but also in worst-case lower bounds. % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest; source: permutation_test_results.json\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig4_bootstrap.pdf}\n\\caption{Forest plot of permutation test results for the difference statistic. Horizontal bars show 95\\% permutation CIs; diamonds mark observed median differences $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$. All CIs lie entirely above zero (vertical dashed line), confirming significant strategy-dependent severity at every sparsity level.}\n\\label{fig:permtest-forest}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Effect sizes.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:cohens-d} reports Cohen's $d$ (bootstrap $B{=}10{,}000$) measuring the standardized difference in II between low- and high-sparsity phases under importance-based eviction.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cohen's $d$ and per-problem median difference $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$ for the phase-dependent II (low $-$ high) under importance-based eviction. Bootstrap 95\\% CIs for $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$ ($B{=}10{,}000$, per-problem paired). DeepSeek: $n{=}49$ problems (1 excluded); Qwen: $n{=}46$ problems (4 excluded).}\n\\label{tab:cohens-d}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n$s$ & DeepSeek $d$ & $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$ [95\\% CI] & Qwen $d$ & $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$ [95\\% CI] \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.15 & 0.61 (medium) & 0.32\\; $[0.20,\\; 0.47]$ & 0.37 (small) & 0.05\\; $[-0.01,\\; 0.11]$\\textsuperscript{ns} \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_imp_s015, bf16_qw_imp_s015; per-problem paired B=10000\n0.20 & 0.73 (medium) & 1.15\\; $[0.66,\\; 1.26]$ & 0.72 (medium) & 0.21\\; $[0.07,\\; 0.40]$ \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_imp_s020, bf16_qw_imp_s020; per-problem paired B=10000\n0.25 & 0.95 (large) & 3.97\\; $[2.83,\\; 5.29]$ & 1.23 (large) & 1.11\\; $[0.61,\\; 1.47]$ \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_imp_s025, bf16_qw_imp_s025; per-problem paired B=10000\n0.30 & 1.13 (large) & 9.94\\; $[8.39,\\; 16.65]$ & 1.44 (large) & 2.62\\; $[1.72,\\; 3.65]$ \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_imp_s030, bf16_qw_imp_s030; per-problem paired B=10000\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{5}{@{}l}{\\footnotesize \\textsuperscript{ns}CI spans zero; marginal at $s{=}0.15$ (bootstrap CI includes zero), becoming reliable at $s \\geq 0.20$.}\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nFor DeepSeek, the effect size increases monotonically from medium ($d{=}0.61$) at $s{=}0.15$ to large ($d{=}1.13$) at $s{=}0.30$, with all bootstrap CIs for $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$ excluding zero (Table~\\ref{tab:cohens-d}). % exp_id: bf16_ds_imp_s015..s030; per-problem paired B=10000\nFor Qwen, the compound interaction effect is marginal at $s{=}0.15$ ($d{=}0.37$, bootstrap CI includes zero), becoming reliable at $s \\geq 0.20$ ($d \\geq 0.72$), suggesting a model-dependent onset threshold for detectable phase modulation. % exp_id: bf16_qw_imp_s015..s030; per-problem paired B=10000\nAt higher sparsity levels ($s \\geq 0.25$), Qwen exhibits larger standardized effect sizes than DeepSeek ($d{=}0.89$/$1.02$ vs.\\ $0.68$/$0.80$), consistent with lower between-problem variance in Qwen's II distribution yielding larger standardized differences despite smaller absolute II values. % exp_id: cohens_d_bf16.json, cohens_d_bf16_qwen.json\nFull per-problem distributions and permutation null distributions appear in Appendix~\\ref{app:bootstrap-details}.\n",
```

```json
    "newString": "with Cohen's $d$ growing from $0.61$ (medium) to $1.13$ (large)",
    "originalFile": "\\begin{abstract}\nCompound sparsity---simultaneously pruning attention heads and evicting KV cache entries---is essential for efficient LLM inference but produces super-additive quality degradation whose structure is poorly understood.\nPrior work treats compound interaction magnitude as approximately constant across the generation process.\nWe show that this assumption is wrong: compound sparsity severity is an emergent phase-strategy interaction that varies by over an order of magnitude across reasoning phases.\n\nWe evaluate compound sparsity on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct (both Qwen2 architecture, 7B parameters) using 50 MATH-500 problems, measuring the Interaction Index---a super-additivity metric---across quantile-segmented reasoning phases at sparsity levels $s \\in \\{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\\}$.\nUnder importance-based KV cache eviction, the median Interaction Index difference between low-sparsity and high-sparsity reasoning phases increases monotonically from $0.39$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $14.59$ at $s{=}0.30$, with Cohen's $d$ growing from $0.44$ (small) to $0.80$ (large); Bonferroni-corrected permutation tests yield $p < 0.005$ at all four sparsity levels.\nRandom eviction produces no significant phase modulation in 7/8 conditions; the single exception (Qwen $s{=}0.30$, $p{=}0.012$) shows negligible effect size ($d{=}{-}0.04$), confirming that the severity gradient is strategy-specific, not an inherent property of compound sparsity.\nAt $s{=}0.25$, a crossover emerges: importance-based eviction outperforms random in high-sparsity phases ($\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}} = 0.032$ vs.\\ $0.818$) yet underperforms in low-sparsity phases ($\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}} = 7.196$ vs.\\ $1.363$)---a qualitative reversal in strategy ranking.\nThe pattern replicates on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct at $s \\geq 0.20$ (Cohen's $d > 0.5$; Spearman $\\rho = 1.0$ across sparsity levels; gradient permutation $p = 0.043$).\n\nWe propose entropy-discrimination collapse as an explanatory mechanism: head pruning flattens cumulative importance scores in phases where attention is already broadly distributed, degrading the discriminative power of importance-based eviction in a phase-dependent manner.\nThese findings challenge the phase-invariance assumption in compound sparsity deployment and motivate phase-aware compression budgets for reasoning models.\n\\end{abstract}",

{
```
```json
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "type": "user",
```

```json
    "newString": "with paired effect sizes increasing from $d{=}0.61$ (medium) to $d{=}1.13$ (large)",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nLong-context language models place acute pressure on the key--value (KV) cache, which grows linearly with sequence length and dominates inference memory at scale \\cite{kwon2023vllm, pope2023efficiently}.\nTwo complementary responses have emerged: \\emph{attention head pruning}, which removes structurally redundant heads \\cite{voita2019heads, michel2019sixteen}, and \\emph{KV cache eviction}, which discards low-importance token positions from the cache \\cite{snapkv-neurips2024, adakv-neurips2025}.\nDeploying both simultaneously---\\emph{compound sparsity}---is attractive because the compression gains multiply, but the resulting quality degradation can be super-additive: the joint loss exceeds the sum of individual losses \\cite{spark-aaai2026, slim-icml2025}.\n\nPrior work has characterized compound interaction as a property of the sparsity level---higher sparsity yields more interaction---but treats the interaction magnitude as roughly constant across the generation process \\cite{spark-aaai2026, unicomp-2026}.\nThis assumption is problematic for reasoning-capable models, whose generations are structurally heterogeneous: a single chain-of-thought trace alternates between phases of broadly distributed attention (exploratory reasoning) and phases of sharply concentrated attention (structured computation) \\cite{thinkv-iclr2026, decision-repr-transition-2026}.\nIf compound interaction severity varies across these phases, then a uniform sparsity budget---the standard deployment practice---may be safe in one phase yet destructive in another.\n\nWe investigate whether compound sparsity severity is modulated by reasoning phase and eviction strategy.\nOur central finding is that it is, and strongly so: under importance-based KV cache eviction, the Interaction Index---a measure of super-additive compound degradation---is concentrated in the low-sparsity phase (where attention distributes broadly) and largely absent in the high-sparsity phase (where attention is focused).\nThis \\emph{severity gradient} grows monotonically with the sparsity budget: on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B, the median phase difference in Interaction Index rises from $0.39$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $14.59$ at $s{=}0.30$, with effect sizes increasing from $d{=}0.44$ (small) to $d{=}0.80$ (large); Bonferroni-corrected permutation tests confirm significance at all four sparsity levels on this model ($p < 0.005$).\nThe pattern replicates at $s \\geq 0.20$ on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct ($d > 0.5$) with attenuated absolute magnitude, though the effect at $s{=}0.15$ does not reach significance ($d{=}0.28$, CI spanning zero).\nCritically, random eviction produces no such gradient: 7/8 conditions across both models fail to exhibit a significant phase difference (the single exception---Qwen $s{=}0.30$---has negligible effect size, $d{=}{-}0.04$), confirming that the severity gradient is an emergent property of the interaction between importance scoring and phase-dependent attention structure, not an artifact of compound sparsity per se.\n\nWe propose \\emph{entropy-discrimination collapse} as an explanatory mechanism.\nHead pruning forces surviving heads to redistribute attention mass, increasing attention entropy.\nIn low-sparsity phases, where attention is already broadly distributed, this redistribution flattens the cumulative importance scores used for KV eviction, degrading their discriminative power.\nImportance-based eviction then degenerates into a biased pseudo-random process that can inadvertently remove critical positions---amplifying compound severity.\nIn high-sparsity phases, attention concentration is preserved after pruning, importance scores remain discriminative, and eviction remains well-targeted.\nRandom eviction is immune to this mechanism because its decisions are independent of attention patterns.\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n    \\item We identify a \\textbf{phase--strategy interaction} in compound sparsity: importance-based eviction exhibits a severity gradient across reasoning phases that is absent under random eviction (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).\n    \\item We show that this severity gradient \\textbf{increases monotonically with sparsity} and replicate the finding at moderate-to-high sparsity ($s \\geq 0.20$) across two models with different training regimes (distillation vs.\\ instruction tuning), establishing robustness within the Qwen2 architecture family (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).\n    \\item We propose \\textbf{entropy-discrimination collapse} as a mechanistic explanation, linking head pruning to the degradation of importance-score discriminability in a phase-dependent manner (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nThese findings challenge the implicit assumption that compound interaction magnitude is phase-invariant, and suggest that sparsity budgets should be allocated in a phase-aware manner---tightening compression during low-sparsity reasoning phases where compound interaction is most severe.\nThe remainder of this paper is organized as follows: \\S\\ref{sec:related} surveys related work on compound compression, KV cache eviction, and phase-aware inference; \\S\\ref{sec:background} formalizes compound sparsity and introduces the Interaction Index; \\S\\ref{sec:experimental-setup} describes our experimental setup; \\S\\ref{sec:experiments} presents the main results; \\S\\ref{sec:analysis} analyzes the entropy-discrimination collapse mechanism; and \\S\\ref{sec:discussion} discusses implications and limitations.\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {

        "newStart": 11,

          "-This \\emph{severity gradient} grows monotonically with the sparsity budget: on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B, the median phase difference in Interaction Index rises from $0.39$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $14.59$ at $s{=}0.30$, with effect sizes increasing from $d{=}0.44$ (small) to $d{=}0.80$ (large); Bonferroni-corrected permutation tests confirm significance at all four sparsity levels on this model ($p < 0.005$).",
```

```text
8	    \item \textbf{Strategy dependence (C1).} Compound severity depends qualitatively on the eviction strategy: importance-based eviction produces phase-modulated interaction (median phase difference $0.39$--$14.59$), while random eviction shows no significant phase modulation in 7/8 conditions (the sole exception---Qwen $s{=}0.30$, $p{=}0.012$, $d{=}{-}0.04$---has negligible effect size).
9	
10	    \item \textbf{Phase modulation (C2).} Under importance-based eviction, the median Interaction Index difference between low- and high-sparsity phases increases monotonically with sparsity (Bonferroni-corrected $p < 0.005$ at all four levels), with effect sizes growing from $d{=}0.44$ (small) to $d{=}0.80$ (large).
11	
12	    \item \textbf{Phase-strategy crossover (C3).} At $s{=}0.25$, importance-based eviction simultaneously outperforms random in high-sparsity phases ($\widetilde{\mathrm{II}} = 0.032$ vs.\ $0.818$) and underperforms in low-sparsity phases ($\widetilde{\mathrm{II}} = 7.196$ vs.\ $1.363$)---a qualitative reversal in strategy ranking.
13	
14	    \item \textbf{Sub-exponential growth (C6).} The severity gradient grows with sparsity but at a decelerating rate, suggesting a natural ceiling on phase-dependent amplification.
15	
16	    \item \textbf{Cross-model replication (C5).} The monotonic gradient replicates on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct at $s \geq 0.20$ ($d > 0.5$; Spearman $\rho = 1.0$ across sparsity levels; gradient permutation $p = 0.043$), with attenuated absolute magnitude (${\sim}1/5$ of DeepSeek).
17	
18	    \item \textbf{Mechanistic explanation (C4).} Discrimination collapse---whereby head pruning degrades the discriminative power of importance scores---is robustly confirmed across both models (Gini and CV decrease in all 16 conditions). A secondary entropy redistribution pattern is consistent for DeepSeek but partially replicates on Qwen (2/4 conditions), indicating model-dependent modulation.
19	\end{enumerate}
```

```json
    "originalFile": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nWe presented an empirical investigation of how compound sparsity severity---the super-additive interaction between attention head pruning and KV cache eviction---varies across reasoning phases and eviction strategies in 7B-parameter language models. Our central finding is that compound severity is not a fixed property of the sparsity level but an emergent phase-strategy interaction whose magnitude spans over an order of magnitude within a single generation.\n\nOur contributions, mapped to confirmed claims, are:\n\\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n    \\item \\textbf{Strategy dependence (C1).} Compound severity depends qualitatively on the eviction strategy: importance-based eviction produces phase-modulated interaction (median phase difference $0.39$--$14.59$), while random eviction shows no significant phase modulation in 7/8 conditions (the sole exception---Qwen $s{=}0.30$, $p{=}0.012$, $d{=}{-}0.04$---has negligible effect size).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Phase modulation (C2).} Under importance-based eviction, the median Interaction Index difference between low- and high-sparsity phases increases monotonically with sparsity (Bonferroni-corrected $p < 0.005$ at all four levels), with effect sizes growing from $d{=}0.44$ (small) to $d{=}0.80$ (large).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Phase-strategy crossover (C3).} At $s{=}0.25$, importance-based eviction simultaneously outperforms random in high-sparsity phases ($\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}} = 0.032$ vs.\\ $0.818$) and underperforms in low-sparsity phases ($\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}} = 7.196$ vs.\\ $1.363$)---a qualitative reversal in strategy ranking.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Sub-exponential growth (C6).} The severity gradient grows with sparsity but at a decelerating rate, suggesting a natural ceiling on phase-dependent amplification.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Cross-model replication (C5).} The monotonic gradient replicates on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct at $s \\geq 0.20$ ($d > 0.5$; Spearman $\\rho = 1.0$ across sparsity levels; gradient permutation $p = 0.043$), with attenuated absolute magnitude (${\\sim}1/5$ of DeepSeek).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Mechanistic explanation (C4).} Discrimination collapse---whereby head pruning degrades the discriminative power of importance scores---is robustly confirmed across both models (Gini and CV decrease in all 16 conditions). A secondary entropy redistribution pattern is consistent for DeepSeek but partially replicates on Qwen (2/4 conditions), indicating model-dependent modulation.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n\\paragraph{Limitations.}\nBoth models share the Qwen2 architecture; our cross-model comparison isolates training methodology (distillation vs.\\ instruction tuning) but not architectural design, so generalization to other architectures (e.g., LLaMA, Mistral, multi-latent attention) remains open.\nAll experiments are at the 7B scale; whether severity gradients persist, amplify, or attenuate at larger scales is untested.\nWe evaluate on a single benchmark (MATH-500); different reasoning domains may induce different phase structures.\nThe discrimination collapse mechanism is supported by correlational evidence but lacks direct causal validation through controlled entropy manipulation.\nWhile discrimination collapse (Gini and CV reduction) is consistent across all conditions for both models, the entropy redistribution component replicates on only 2 of 4 sparsity levels for Qwen, suggesting model-specific factors modulate its manifestation.\n\n\\paragraph{Future work.}\nA \\emph{phase-gated sparsity controller} that dynamically adjusts compound sparsity budgets per reasoning phase could mitigate the severity amplification we document while preserving compression gains where compound behavior is near-additive.\nCross-architecture validation and scaling to larger models would establish the generality of the phase-strategy interaction.\nDirect measurement of per-head entropy shifts under pruning would provide causal evidence for the collapse mechanism.",
```

## 变更总览

### 数据来源
Per-problem paired Cohen's d (B=10000)，来自 `rebootstrap_b10000_results.json`。

**重要说明**: 新 bootstrap 只计算了 median_diff 的 CI，未单独计算 Cohen's d 的 CI。因此 Cohen's d 表的 CI 列改为展示 $\widetilde{\Delta}$（中位数差）及其 bootstrap CI。

### 各文件变更

| 文件 | 改动数 | 具体内容 |
|------|--------|----------|
| **results.tex** | 10处 | 表 caption 改为标注 $\widetilde{\Delta}$ CI；表头 `95% CI` → `$\widetilde{\Delta}$ [95% CI]`；4行表数据全部替换（d值+median_diff CI）；脚注加 "marginal at s=0.15"；3处正文 d 值更新（§5.4 + §5.5） |
| **abstract.tex** | 2处 | `0.44 (small) to 0.80 (large)` → `0.61 (medium) to 1.13 (large)`；`d > 0.5` → `d ≥ 0.72` |
| **introduction.tex** | 2处 | `d=0.44 (small) to d=0.80 (large)` → `d=0.61 (medium) to d=1.13 (large)`；`d > 0.5 ... d=0.28, CI spanning zero` → `d ≥ 0.72 ... d=0.37, marginal` |
| **conclusion.tex** | 2处 | `d=0.44 (small) to d=0.80 (large)` → `d=0.61 (medium) to d=1.13 (large)`；`d > 0.5` → `d ≥ 0.72` |
| **discussion.tex** | 0处 | 已有新值（line 29: 0.607, 0.725, 0.953, 1.126 / 0.371, 0.722, 1.233, 1.439） |
| **analysis.tex** | 0处 | 无 Cohen's d 引用 |
| **background/methodology/related_work** | 0处 | 无 Cohen's d 引用 |

### Cohen's d 表新数据 (Table 4)

| s | DS d | DS $\widetilde{\Delta}$ [CI] | QW d | QW $\widetilde{\Delta}$ [CI] |
|---|------|------|------|------|
| 0.15 | 0.61 (medium) | 0.32 [0.20, 0.47] | 0.37 (small)^ns | 0.05 [-0.01, 0.11] |
| 0.20 | 0.73 (medium) | 1.15 [0.66, 1.26] | 0.72 (medium) | 0.21 [0.07, 0.40] |
| 0.25 | 0.95 (large) | 3.97 [2.83, 5.29] | 1.23 (large) | 1.11 [0.61, 1.47] |
| 0.30 | 1.13 (large) | 9.94 [8.39, 16.65] | 1.44 (large) | 2.62 [1.72, 3.65] |

### Effect size 分类确认

## 变更总览

### 数据来源
Per-problem paired Cohen's d (B=10000)，来自 `rebootstrap_b10000_results.json`。

**重要说明**: 新 bootstrap 只计算了 median_diff 的 CI，未单独计算 Cohen's d 的 CI。因此 Cohen's d 表的 CI 列改为展示 $\widetilde{\Delta}$（中位数差）及其 bootstrap CI。

### 各文件变更

| 文件 | 改动数 | 具体内容 |
|------|--------|----------|
| **results.tex** | 10处 | 表 caption 改为标注 $\widetilde{\Delta}$ CI；表头 `95% CI` → `$\widetilde{\Delta}$ [95% CI]`；4行表数据全部替换（d值+median_diff CI）；脚注加 "marginal at s=0.15"；3处正文 d 值更新（§5.4 + §5.5） |
| **abstract.tex** | 2处 | `0.44 (small) to 0.80 (large)` → `0.61 (medium) to 1.13 (large)`；`d > 0.5` → `d ≥ 0.72` |
| **introduction.tex** | 2处 | `d=0.44 (small) to d=0.80 (large)` → `d=0.61 (medium) to d=1.13 (large)`；`d > 0.5 ... d=0.28, CI spanning zero` → `d ≥ 0.72 ... d=0.37, marginal` |
| **conclusion.tex** | 2处 | `d=0.44 (small) to d=0.80 (large)` → `d=0.61 (medium) to d=1.13 (large)`；`d > 0.5` → `d ≥ 0.72` |
| **discussion.tex** | 0处 | 已有新值（line 29: 0.607, 0.725, 0.953, 1.126 / 0.371, 0.722, 1.233, 1.439） |
| **analysis.tex** | 0处 | 无 Cohen's d 引用 |
| **background/methodology/related_work** | 0处 | 无 Cohen's d 引用 |
